Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I still feel like they’re hiding it or using it to watch what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt confused or not fully aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed feeling confused like that. Sometimes I’m stressed and things feel like a lot, but I don’t think I’ve been unaware of what’s going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less interested in being around other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I’ll go through stretches where I just don’t call people back. I don’t really have anything specific to tell you about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a seizure?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I don’t remember ever having one or being told I had one.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your speech or thoughts seem jumbled or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Nobody’s said that to me directly, at least not that I can remember. Sometimes I lose my train of thought, but I’m not sure if it comes across as jumbled to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain illness or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I haven’t been diagnosed with anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to do your job been getting worse since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been getting worse. I’ve made mistakes at the shop, and they pulled me off the high-voltage EV work because people were worried I wasn’t safe doing it. And at home it got bad too—there was so much fighting that my husband ended up taking me in for an emergency evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face seems less expressive or that your voice sounds flat?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anyone’s said my face or voice exactly, but I know I kind of shut down. I don’t call people back, I stopped going to the church potlucks I used to help with, and sometimes I just sit at home or in the bay staring at my tools like I can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started with the voice, and around the same time I started feeling like I was being tracked. It’s been going on most of this past year.\n\nDoctor: Were you using any drugs around the time the voice first started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t remember anything specific around when it started. What do you mean by drugs—like street drugs, or medications?\n\nDoctor: Have you used any street drugs like meth, cocaine, or hallucinogens?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clear answer for that—nothing specific comes to mind right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is tracking you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it sounds far-fetched, but I’m convinced my husband and the shop owner are tracking me through the EV chargers and the security cameras at the shop. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I still feel like they’re hiding it or using it to watch what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt confused or not fully aware of what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed feeling confused like that. Sometimes I’m stressed and things feel like a lot, but I don’t think I’ve been unaware of what’s going on around me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less interested in being around other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I’ll go through stretches where I just don’t call people back. I skip the church potlucks I used to help with, and I’ll just sit at home or in the shop bay staring at my tools like I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you used methamphetamine?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what you mean exactly—like using it recently, or ever? I don’t really have anything specific to tell you about that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a seizure?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of. I don’t remember ever having one or being told I had one.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your speech or thoughts seem jumbled or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Nobody’s said that to me directly, at least not that I can remember. Sometimes I lose my train of thought, but I’m not sure if it comes across as jumbled to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any brain illness or neurological condition?\n\nPatient: No, not that I’m aware of. I haven’t been diagnosed with anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to do your job been getting worse since these experiences began?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been getting worse. I’ve made mistakes at the shop, and they pulled me off the high-voltage EV work because people were worried I wasn’t safe doing it. And at home it got bad too—there was so much fighting that my husband ended up taking me in for an emergency evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face seems less expressive or that your voice sounds flat?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anyone’s said my face or voice exactly, but I know I kind of shut down. I don’t call people back, I stopped going to the church potlucks I used to help with, and sometimes I just sit at home or in the bay staring at my tools like I can’t make myself care.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a time when you felt unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. There were times for weeks where I’d only sleep maybe two or three hours, and I’d wake up before dawn already thinking about the garage. I didn’t feel tired like I should’ve—I felt like going back to sleep would just be wasting time.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you slept only two or three hours, did you also feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes I did feel really high, like I could fix every car in town and nobody could keep up with me. But it could switch fast—if my husband asked about dishes or somebody at work moved my socket set, I’d just blow up, like they were trying to hold me back.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard the voice during times when you were not feeling unusually energized or unusually down?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when things were kind of quieter for a few weeks—like I was sleeping more and wasn’t making all these big plans—I still heard that voice in the garage. And I still thought my husband and my boss were tracking me. It wasn’t only when I was really up or really down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I’ve had times where I felt low and just not right, but I don’t know if it was most of the day nearly every day for two full weeks. I’d have to think about it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
